\title{\textbf{Simon's Multidimensional $L^2$ Conjecture}\\[3pt]
\large Operator-valued reciprocal Riesz contraction and the radial short-time problem}
\author{Siam Siraji\\[3pt]
\small The University of Adelaide, Adelaide, South Australia, Australia\\
\small \href{mailto:siam.siraji@adelaide.edu.au}{siam.siraji@adelaide.edu.au}}
\date{24 July 2026}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
We study reciprocal transfer recursions of the form
\[
 \Phi_z(H)=(a\Id+zbH)^{-1}(aH+\bar z b\Id),
 \qquad a^2-b^2=1,
\]
which arise in finite-channel approximations to multidimensional
Schr\"odinger scattering.  A proposed one-cell fractional contraction with a
dimension-independent gap is false: root-of-unity phases make the gap vanish
as the dimension grows.  We prove the correct replacement.  In every fixed
dimension there is strict contraction, and a finite product of fixed-height
scalar cells collapses to one effective Poisson barrier.  Positive drift of
its hyperbolic height and Haar uniformity of its centre yield uniform
fractional contraction on compact families of singular operator-valued
spectral measures.  Deterministic lacunary phases, compact-output stability,
and a dovetailed no-escape argument remove the apparent circular dependence
between group length and Galerkin dimension.

We then examine physical realization by scalar angular multipliers on
\(\mathbb S^2\).  A shrinking-window carrier construction produces
drift-decorated population swaps with full-operator control and arbitrarily
small quadratic cost on each prescribed nonempty window.  Several natural
globalizations are ruled out: no fixed grouped length is uniform over all
prior reciprocal histories; adjacent-band multiband tilings have a positive
Zakharov--Shabat energy floor; direct scaled slabs and every bounded-depth
multiscale cascade freeze spatial densities; and uniformly smooth
logarithmic-shell controls converge to phase multipliers.  A surviving
logarithmic construction must be nonperturbative, have angular degree
comparable to the shell radius, and control a growing resonant cluster
collectively.

The operator-valued contraction theorem is unconditional.  Its insertion
into the full radial Schr\"odinger problem is reduced to one explicit
short-angular-time radialization problem.  Until that theorem is proved, the
global physical contraction and the resulting counterexample to Simon's
multidimensional \(L^2\) conjecture remain conditional; no resolution of the
conjecture is claimed.
\end{abstract}

\noindent\textbf{2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.}
Primary 47A55, 42A55; Secondary 81Q10, 93B05, 35P25.

\noindent\textbf{Keywords.}
operator-valued Riesz product, reciprocal M\"obius map, singular spectral
measure, Schr\"odinger control, ensemble controllability, radialization.

\medskip
\noindent
\fcolorbox{open}{orange!5}{\begin{minipage}{0.94\textwidth}
\textbf{Scope of the claims.}
The operator-valued contraction, fixed-dimensional realizations, local
shrinking-window carrier theorem, and stated no-go results are presented as
unconditional.  Problem~\ref{prob:short-time-radial-compiler} is the remaining
conditional input.  Consequently the global physical contraction and any
counterexample to Simon's conjecture are not claimed.
\end{minipage}}

\section{The correction in one line}

Fix $\rho>0$ and put
\begin{equation}
 a=\cosh\rho,\qquad b=\sinh\rho,\qquad t=\frac ba\in(0,1).
 \label{eq:ab}
\end{equation}
For $H\in\U(n)$ and $z\in\T$, the ideal reciprocal factor is
\begin{equation}
 F_z(H)=(a\Id+zbH)^{-1}.
 \label{eq:F}
\end{equation}
The originally requested estimate had one $q_\beta<1$ for all $n$.
The correct statement is
\begin{equation}
 \int_\T \tr\!\left[(F_z(H)^*AF_z(H))^\beta\right]\dd m(z)
 \leq \kappa_{\beta,n}\tr(A^\beta),
 \qquad \kappa_{\beta,n}<1,
 \label{eq:correct-contraction}
\end{equation}
where $0<\beta<1$, $A\geq0$, and the constant is uniform in $H$ and $A$
but necessarily depends on $n$.

This dependence is harmless at the scale-ledger level.  At generation $q$,
with $n_q=\dim\mathcal D_q$, choose a repetition count $r_q$ so large that
\begin{equation}
 \kappa_{\beta,n_q}^{r_q}\leq 2^{-4q}.
 \label{eq:repeat}
\end{equation}
Then choose the concentration depth and physical accuracy of each of the
$r_q$ cells so that their total $X_3$ cost and total factorisation error are
at most $2^{-q}$.  The radii may be enormous; existence only requires that
the choices are finite at each generation.

\section{Finite-dimensional strict contraction}

Let $m$ denote normalized Haar measure on $\T$.

\begin{lemma}[Hardy normalization]
\label{lem:hardy}
For every finite-dimensional unitary $H$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_\T F_z(H)^*F_z(H)\dd m(z)=\Id.
 \label{eq:hardy}
\end{equation}
The same identity holds with $F_zF_z^*$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Write $C=tH$.  Since $a^{-2}=1-t^2$,
\[
 F_z=a^{-1}(\Id+zC)^{-1}.
\]
Expanding in nonnegative powers of $z$, applying Parseval, and telescoping
gives
\[
 \int_\T F_z^*F_z\dd m(z)
 =(1-t^2)\sum_{j\geq0}t^{2j}\Id=\Id.
\]
Because $F_z$ is a function of the normal operator $H$, it is normal, so the
second identity follows as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Uniform strict contraction in every fixed dimension]
\label{thm:finite-contraction}
Let $0<\beta<1$ and $n<\infty$.  There is a number
$\kappa_{\beta,n}=\kappa_{\beta,n}(\rho)<1$ such that
\eqref{eq:correct-contraction} holds for every $H\in\U(n)$ and every
positive $n\times n$ matrix $A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a unit vector $u$, set
\begin{equation}
 s_{H,u}(z)=\|F_z(H)^*u\|^2.
 \label{eq:s}
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem:hardy} gives $\int s_{H,u}\dd m=1$.  We first prove the
strict, uniform rank-one estimate
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{H\in\U(n),\ \|u\|=1}
 \int_\T s_{H,u}(z)^\beta\dd m(z)<1.
 \label{eq:rank-one-uniform}
\end{equation}

Let $\nu_{H,u}$ be the spectral measure of $H$ at $u$.  The Poisson-kernel
identity
\begin{equation}
 |a+b\zeta|^{-2}
 =\frac{1-t^2}{|1+t\zeta|^2}
 =1+2\sum_{k\geq1}(-t)^k\operatorname{Re}(\zeta^k)
 \label{eq:poisson}
\end{equation}
shows that
\begin{equation}
 s_{H,u}(z)
 =1+2\sum_{k\geq1}(-t)^k
   \operatorname{Re}\!\left(z^k\langle u,H^ku\rangle\right).
 \label{eq:s-fourier}
\end{equation}
If $s_{H,u}$ were constant, then
$\langle u,H^ku\rangle=0$ for every $k\geq1$.  But $\nu_{H,u}$ is a
probability measure supported on at most $n$ points.  If its distinct support
points are $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_r$ with positive weights
$p_1,\ldots,p_r$, then the equations
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^r p_j\lambda_j^k=0,
 \qquad k=1,\ldots,r,
\]
form an invertible Vandermonde system and would force every $p_j$ to vanish.
This is impossible.  Hence $s_{H,u}$ is nonconstant.  Strict concavity of
$x^\beta$, together with $\int s_{H,u}=1$, gives
\[
 \int_\T s_{H,u}^\beta\dd m<1.
\]
The integrand is uniformly bounded because
$a-b\leq|a+zb\lambda|\leq a+b$.  The displayed functional is continuous on
the compact space $\U(n)\times\{u:\|u\|=1\}$.  Its supremum is therefore
strictly below one.  Denote that supremum by $\kappa_{\beta,n}$.

Now diagonalize
$A=\sum_j\lambda_j|u_j\rangle\langle u_j|$.  Rotfel'd's trace
subadditivity for $0<\beta<1$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \tr[(F_z^*AF_z)^\beta]
 &\leq \sum_j
 \tr\!\left[
   \bigl(\lambda_j|F_z^*u_j\rangle\langle F_z^*u_j|\bigr)^\beta
 \right]\\
 &=\sum_j\lambda_j^\beta s_{H,u_j}(z)^\beta.
\end{align*}
Integrating and applying the rank-one bound proves
\eqref{eq:correct-contraction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why this is the right matrix theorem]
The proof does not assume that the spectrum of $H$ lies in a short arc and
does not assume that $A$ commutes with $H$.  It therefore handles precisely
the phase spreading and rotation into moving angular directions that defeat
the fixed-probe entropy argument.
\end{remark}

\section{A fixed contraction independent of dimension is impossible}

\begin{proposition}[Root-of-unity obstruction]
\label{prop:no-dim-free}
For fixed $0<\beta<1$ and $\rho>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{n\to\infty}\kappa_{\beta,n}=1.
 \label{eq:kappa-to-one}
\end{equation}
In particular, the proposed version of Lemma B with one fixed
$q_\beta<1$ for all growing targeted blocks is \falseclaim.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Let
\[
 H_n=\operatorname{diag}(1,e^{2\pi i/n},\ldots,e^{2\pi i(n-1)/n}),
 \qquad
 u_n=n^{-1/2}(1,\ldots,1)^T,
\]
and let $A_n=|u_n\rangle\langle u_n|$.  Then
\begin{align}
 s_n(e^{i\phi})
 &=\|F_{e^{i\phi}}(H_n)^*u_n\|^2\notag\\
 &=\frac1n\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}
 |a+be^{i(\phi+2\pi j/n)}|^{-2}\notag\\
 &=1+2\sum_{r\geq1}(-t)^{rn}\cos(rn\phi).
 \label{eq:root-average}
\end{align}
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \|s_n-1\|_{L^\infty(\T)}
 \leq \frac{2t^n}{1-t^n}\longrightarrow0.
 \label{eq:root-uniform}
\end{equation}
Since $\tr(A_n^\beta)=1$,
\[
 \int_\T\tr[(F_z(H_n)^*A_nF_z(H_n))^\beta]\dd m(z)
 =\int_\T s_n(z)^\beta\dd m(z)\longrightarrow1.
\]
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-contraction} gives the reverse inequality
$\kappa_{\beta,n}<1$ for each $n$, proving~\eqref{eq:kappa-to-one}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Interpretation]
The obstruction is coherent phase averaging.  A vector spread uniformly
over many reciprocal eigenphases sees an almost constant average of the
Poisson kernel, so one random circle phase produces almost no fractional
gain.  Repetition, rather than a nonexistent dimension-free gap, is the
correct repair.
\end{remark}

\section{Strong barriers for singular phases}

The preceding dimension obstruction fixes $\rho$.  Physical reciprocal
phases have additional structure, and allowing the hyperbolic angle to grow
gives a stronger route.

\begin{theorem}[Poisson contraction for a singular reciprocal phase]
\label{thm:strong-barrier}
Let $H$ be unitary on a separable Hilbert space and let $u$ be a unit vector
whose spectral measure $\nu_{H,u}$ is singular with respect to Haar measure
on $\T$.  For $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{equation}
 \lim_{\rho\to\infty}
 \int_\T
 \left\|
  (\cosh\rho\,\Id+z\sinh\rho\,H)^{-*}u
 \right\|^{2\beta}\dd m(z)=0.
 \label{eq:strong-barrier-limit}
\end{equation}
If $\mathfrak C$ is a compact family of pairs $(H,u)$ in operator norm
times vector norm, and every associated spectral measure is singular, then
the convergence is uniform on $\mathfrak C$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Put $t=\tanh\rho$.  Up to the harmless rotation $z\mapsto-z$, the squared
norm in~\eqref{eq:strong-barrier-limit} is the Poisson integral
\[
 s_{t,\nu}(z)=(P_t*\nu)(z).
\]
The radial boundary theorem gives $s_{t,\nu}(z)\to0$ for Haar-almost every
$z$ when $\nu$ is singular.  Moreover, for every measurable $E\subset\T$,
\[
 \int_Es_{t,\nu}^\beta\dd m
 \leq m(E)^{1-\beta}
      \left(\int_Es_{t,\nu}\dd m\right)^\beta
 \leq m(E)^{1-\beta}.
\]
Thus the fractional powers are uniformly integrable, and
\eqref{eq:strong-barrier-limit} follows.

For uniformity, define
$Q_t(\nu)=\int(P_t*\nu)^\beta\dd m$.  If $0<t_1<t_2<1$, then
$P_{t_1}=P_{t_1/t_2}*P_{t_2}$, and concavity gives
$Q_{t_1}(\nu)\geq Q_{t_2}(\nu)$.  For fixed $t<1$, $Q_t$ is continuous in
the weak topology of probability measures.  The spectral-measure map is
continuous on $\mathfrak C$, so its image is compact.  Pointwise monotone
convergence to the continuous limit zero is uniform by Dini's theorem.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Finite probe bundles need no dimension-free gap]
\label{cor:strong-bundle}
Let $\mathfrak C$ be a compact unit-vector bundle containing the ranges of
the finite-rank positive matrices $A(k)$ targeted in one generation.
If every reciprocal phase spectral measure on $\mathfrak C$ is singular,
then, for any $\delta>0$, one may choose a finite $\rho$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \int_\T\tr[(F_z(H(k))^*A(k)F_z(H(k)))^\beta]\dd m(z)
 \leq\delta\,\tr(A(k)^\beta)
 \label{eq:strong-matrix-contraction}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $k$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Use the spectral decomposition of $A(k)$, Rotfel'd subadditivity, and the
uniform part of Theorem~\ref{thm:strong-barrier}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Compact partial potentials have singular boundary phase]
\label{rem:phase-criterion}
Let $V$ be real, smooth, and compactly supported in the exterior radial
problem, and let
\[
 \Theta_V(k)=J_V(k)^{-1}\overline{J_V(k)},\qquad
 \Theta_0(k)=J_0(k)^{-1}\overline{J_0(k)}.
\]
For every $k>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \Theta_V(k)-\Theta_0(k)\in\mathfrak S_1(L^2(\mathbb S^2)).
 \label{eq:phase-trace-class}
\end{equation}
Consequently $\Theta_V(k)$ has no absolutely continuous spectral subspace,
and every one of its vector spectral measures is singular with respect to
Haar measure on $\T$.  On compact positive energy intervals the boundary
phases are norm-continuous, so Corollary~\ref{cor:strong-bundle} applies
uniformly to every compact finite-probe bundle.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use the free spectral representation for the exterior Dirichlet problem.
The scattering matrix of the pair $(H_0+V,H_0)$ is, up to the fixed free
incoming/outgoing diagonal factors dictated by the Jost normalization,
\[
 \mathsf S_V(k)=\Theta_V(k)\Theta_0(k)^*.
\]
The stationary representation writes
$\mathsf S_V(k)-\Id$ as an integral operator on $\mathbb S^2$ whose kernel is
the on-shell scattering amplitude.  Compact support permits differentiation
of the generalized eigenfunctions in both angular variables to every order;
the kernel is therefore smooth.  A smoothing operator on the compact
two-dimensional sphere is trace class.  Multiplication on the right by the
unitary $\Theta_0(k)$ proves~\eqref{eq:phase-trace-class}.

The free phase $\Theta_0(k)$ is diagonal in spherical harmonics and hence has
pure point spectrum.  The unitary Kato--Rosenblum theorem now implies that
the absolutely continuous parts of $\Theta_V(k)$ and $\Theta_0(k)$ are
unitarily equivalent, so the former vanishes.  Standard stationary
dependence away from zero gives norm continuity in $k$ on compact intervals.
\end{proof}

\section{Exact operator-valued Riesz singularity on the phase torus}

The next theorem shows that the corrected finite-dimensional contraction is
already sufficient in the ideal independent-phase model.

\begin{theorem}[Operator-valued reciprocal Riesz martingale]
\label{thm:torus-singularity}
Fix $n<\infty$, $0<\beta<1$, and an invertible matrix $X_0$.  On
$\Omega=\T^{\mathbb N}$ with product Haar measure and its coordinate
filtration, let $H_j$ be any predictable $\U(n)$-valued process and define
\begin{equation}
 F_j=(a\Id+bz_jH_j)^{-1},\qquad
 X_j=F_jX_{j-1},\qquad
 W_j=X_j^*X_j.
 \label{eq:martingale-recursion}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{align}
 \mathbb E(W_j\mid\mathcal F_{j-1})&=W_{j-1},
 \label{eq:matrix-martingale}\\
 \mathbb E\tr(W_j^\beta)&\leq
 \kappa_{\beta,n}^j\tr(W_0^\beta).
 \label{eq:fractional-decay}
\end{align}
The consistent positive matrix measures with cylinder densities $W_j$ have
a nonzero weak limit $\boldsymbol\mu$ which is purely singular with respect
to product Haar measure.  Its trace measure, and hence every scalarization
$u^*\boldsymbol\mu u$, is supported on one common Haar-null set.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Conditioning on the first $j-1$ coordinates and using
Lemma~\ref{lem:hardy} yields
\[
 \mathbb E(W_j\mid\mathcal F_{j-1})
 =X_{j-1}^*\mathbb E(F_j^*F_j)X_{j-1}=W_{j-1}.
\]
The nonzero eigenvalues of $X_j^*X_j$ and $X_jX_j^*$ agree.  Hence, with
$A_{j-1}=X_{j-1}X_{j-1}^*$,
\[
 \tr(W_j^\beta)
 =\tr[(F_jA_{j-1}F_j^*)^\beta].
\]
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-contraction}, applied after replacing $z$ by
$\bar z$ and $H$ by $H^*$, gives the conditional contraction.  Iteration
proves~\eqref{eq:fractional-decay}.

The matrix martingale converges entrywise almost everywhere.  In finite
dimension this implies convergence of its eigenvalues, and Fatou gives
\[
 \mathbb E\tr(W_\infty^\beta)
 \leq\liminf_{j\to\infty}\mathbb E\tr(W_j^\beta)=0.
\]
Thus $W_\infty=0$ almost everywhere.  The martingale consistency defines a
positive matrix measure on $\Omega$ whose absolutely continuous
Radon--Nikodym derivative is $W_\infty$; therefore its absolutely continuous
part vanishes.  Its total matrix mass equals that of $W_0$ and is nonzero.
Finally, positivity gives
$0\leq u^*\boldsymbol\mu(B)u\leq\tr\boldsymbol\mu(B)$, so a null support for
the trace measure is a common null support for every scalarization.
\end{proof}

\section{Deterministic lacunary phases and tail locking}

The torus theorem is probabilistic only in its notation.  Successively large
physical collar frequencies can realize the conditional phase averages on an
energy interval.  To avoid the moving-spike defect, one must preserve not
only fractional moments but also the mass of concentration witnesses chosen
at earlier stages.

\begin{lemma}[One-step deterministic phase placement]
\label{lem:one-step-placement}
Let $K$ be a compact interval, let $X,H$ be measurable matrix functions on
$K$, with $H(k)\in\U(n)$, and put
\[
 W=X^*X,\qquad
 F_z(k)=(a\Id+zbH(k))^{-1},\qquad
 W_z=(F_zX)^*(F_zX).
\]
Given finitely many bounded measurable scalar test functions
$\psi_1,\ldots,\psi_N$ and $\epsilon>0$, there are arbitrarily large real
frequencies $\nu$ such that, with $z(k)=e^{i\nu k}$,
\begin{align}
 \int_K\tr(W_{z(k)}^\beta)\dd k
 &\leq\kappa_{\beta,n}\int_K\tr(W^\beta)\dd k+\epsilon,
 \label{eq:det-fractional-step}\\
 \left|\int_K\psi_\ell(k)
   \bigl(W_{z(k)}(k)-W(k)\bigr)_{rs}\dd k\right|
 &<\epsilon
 \label{eq:det-test-step}
\end{align}
for every $\ell\leq N$ and every matrix entry $(r,s)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
All denominators are bounded below by $a-b>0$.  Hence the integrands are in
$L^1(K;C(\T))$.  Approximate them by finite Fourier sums in $z$.  Every
nonzero Fourier coefficient tends to zero after substituting
$z=e^{i\nu k}$, by the Riemann--Lebesgue lemma.  The zero Fourier coefficient
in~\eqref{eq:det-fractional-step} is bounded by
Theorem~\ref{thm:finite-contraction}; that in
~\eqref{eq:det-test-step} equals $\psi_\ell W$ by
Lemma~\ref{lem:hardy}.  A finite maximum of the required frequency
thresholds proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Deterministic operator-valued Riesz singularity]
\label{thm:deterministic-singularity}
Fix $n<\infty$ and $0<\beta<1$.  Starting from an integrable invertible
matrix function $X_0$ on a compact interval $K$, let
$H_j:K\to\U(n)$ be any predictable sequence.  There are successively
arbitrarily large collar frequencies $\nu_j$ such that the recursion
\begin{equation}
 X_j(k)=
 \bigl(a\Id+be^{i\nu_jk}H_j(k)\bigr)^{-1}X_{j-1}(k),
 \qquad W_j=X_j^*X_j,
 \label{eq:deterministic-recursion}
\end{equation}
has a weak limiting positive matrix measure
$\boldsymbol\mu$ on $K$ which is purely singular with respect to Lebesgue
measure.  The choices can simultaneously satisfy any previously fixed
finite lists of phase-placement inequalities.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose a summable error sequence.  At step $j$, use
Lemma~\ref{lem:one-step-placement} to obtain
\begin{equation}
 I_j:=\int_K\tr(W_j^\beta)\dd k
 \leq\kappa_{\beta,n}I_{j-1}+\epsilon_j.
 \label{eq:det-fractional-recursion}
\end{equation}
Choose the errors so that $I_j\to0$.  At the same step, preserve the first
$j$ members of a fixed countable dense subset of $C(K)$, against every
matrix entry, with summable tail errors.  The corresponding matrix moments
are Cauchy.  Positivity and compactness of $K$ therefore give a weak limiting
positive matrix measure $\boldsymbol\mu$.

It remains to prove singularity rather than only weak convergence.  Select
stages $j_q$ so that $I_{j_q}$ is as small as required below, and write
$w_q=\tr W_{j_q}$.  For a threshold $T_q$,
\begin{align}
 \bigl|\{w_q>T_q\}\bigr|
 &\leq T_q^{-1}\int_Kw_q\dd k,
 \label{eq:det-large-set}\\
 \int_{\{w_q\leq T_q\}}w_q\dd k
 &\leq T_q^{1-\beta}I_{j_q}.
 \label{eq:det-small-density-mass}
\end{align}
First choose $T_q$ large and then $j_q$ so late that there are an open set
$O_q\subset K$ and a continuous function $0\leq\chi_q\leq1$, supported in
$O_q$, for which
\begin{equation}
 |O_q|\leq2^{-q},\qquad
 \int_K(1-\chi_q)w_q\dd k\leq2^{-q}.
 \label{eq:witness-stage}
\end{equation}
This uses~\eqref{eq:det-large-set}--\eqref{eq:det-small-density-mass} and
regularity of the two finite measures involved.

From stage $j_q+1$ onward, permanently include $1-\chi_q$ in the finite test
list in Lemma~\ref{lem:one-step-placement}.  Allocate its later errors so
that their sum is at most $2^{-q}$.  Passing to the weak limit gives
\begin{equation}
 \tr\boldsymbol\mu(K\setminus O_q)\leq2^{1-q}.
 \label{eq:locked-witness}
\end{equation}
After taking a sparse subsequence if necessary, both right-hand sides are
summable.  The set
\[
 S=\liminf_{q\to\infty}O_q
\]
has Lebesgue measure zero, while the Borel--Cantelli argument applied to the
trace measure and~\eqref{eq:locked-witness} gives
$\tr\boldsymbol\mu(K\setminus S)=0$.  Positivity then puts every
scalarization of $\boldsymbol\mu$ on the same null set.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What defeats the oscillating-density counterexample]
Fractional-moment decay alone is not used to pass to the weak limit.  Each
small concentration witness is frozen into every later collar choice.  Thus
future shells preserve its spectral mass up to a summable error, which is
exactly the missing tail-stability input.
\end{remark}

\section{Dense probes without a growing matrix contraction}

The operator-valued construction above is useful, but it is stronger than is
needed to kill the absolutely continuous subspace.  A countable dense family
of scalar probes can be treated by a dovetailed schedule.  Cells aimed at one
probe need only be nonexpansive, in phase mean, for the finitely many other
probes currently being protected.  The exact Hardy identity supplies that
nonexpansion.

Fix a triangular schedule
\[
 1;\quad 1,2;\quad 1,2,3;\quad\ldots
\]
and denote its successive entries by $\tau(j)$.  Thus every positive integer
occurs infinitely often and $\tau(j)\leq j$.

\begin{theorem}[Dovetailed scalar no-escape principle]
\label{thm:scalar-dovetail}
Let $K$ be a compact interval and $0<\beta<1$.  For $p\leq j$, let
$w_{p,j}\geq0$ be integrable densities on $K$, and put
\[
 I_{p,j}=\int_K w_{p,j}^{\beta}\dd k.
\]
Assume that the stages can be chosen with a summable sequence
$\epsilon_j>0$ so that
\begin{align}
 I_{p,j}&\leq I_{p,j-1}+\epsilon_j,
       &&p\leq j,                                      \label{eq:dovetail-nonexpand}\\
 I_{\tau(j),j}&\leq \tfrac12 I_{\tau(j),j-1}+\epsilon_j.\label{eq:dovetail-contract}
\end{align}
Assume also that, for a fixed dense sequence $(\psi_\ell)$ in $C(K)$,
the first $j$ moments of the first $j$ densities are changed by at most
$\epsilon_j$, entrywise:
\begin{equation}
 \left|\int_K\psi_\ell(w_{p,j}-w_{p,j-1})\dd k\right|
 \leq\epsilon_j,
 \qquad p,\ell\leq j.
 \label{eq:dovetail-moments}
\end{equation}
Finally, whenever a continuous witness $0\leq\chi\leq1$ is declared for a
probe $p$, assume it may be included in every later finite test list with a
prescribed summable total future error.

Then the errors and witness declarations may be chosen so that every
$w_{p,j}\dd k$ has a weak limit $\mu_p$, every $\mu_p$ is singular with
respect to Lebesgue measure, and all $\mu_p$ are supported on one common
Lebesgue-null set.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
For a fixed $p$, inequalities~\eqref{eq:dovetail-nonexpand} and
\eqref{eq:dovetail-contract}, along the infinitely many stages at which
$\tau(j)=p$, give $I_{p,j}\to0$ after making the error tail at the $q$th such
stage smaller than $2^{-2q}$.  Equation~\eqref{eq:dovetail-moments} makes
every continuous moment Cauchy, first on the dense sequence and then on all
of $C(K)$ by positivity and the uniform mass bound.  Hence
$w_{p,j}\dd k\rightharpoonup\mu_p$.

Choose stages $j(p,q)$ with $I_{p,j(p,q)}$ sufficiently small.  With
$w=w_{p,j(p,q)}$, first choose a large threshold $T_{p,q}$ and then choose
$j(p,q)$ so late that an open set $O_{p,q}$ and a continuous cutoff
$0\leq\chi_{p,q}\leq1$, supported in $O_{p,q}$, satisfy
\begin{equation}
 |O_{p,q}|\leq2^{-p-q},\qquad
 \int_K(1-\chi_{p,q})w\dd k\leq2^{-p-q}.
 \label{eq:dovetail-witness}
\end{equation}
Indeed,
\[
 |\{w>T_{p,q}\}|\leq T_{p,q}^{-1}\int_Kw\dd k,
 \qquad
 \int_{\{w\leq T_{p,q}\}}w\dd k
 \leq T_{p,q}^{1-\beta}I_{p,j(p,q)}.
\]
Permanently lock $1-\chi_{p,q}$ with total future error at most
$2^{-p-q}$.  Passing to the weak limit gives
\[
 \mu_p(K\setminus O_{p,q})\leq2^{1-p-q}.
\]
Thus $\mu_p$ is supported on
$S_p=\liminf_qO_{p,q}$, and $|S_p|=0$.  The countable union
$S=\bigcup_pS_p$ is still Lebesgue null and supports every $\mu_p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Why scalar probes suffice]
\label{cor:dense-probes}
Let $(\phi_p)$ be dense in the Hilbert space of the Schr\"odinger operator.
If the construction in Theorem~\ref{thm:scalar-dovetail} is realized by their
spectral densities on $K$, then
\[
 P_{\rm ac}(H)\mathbf1_K(H)=0.
\]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Singularity of the spectral measure of $\phi_p$ on $K$ says
$P_{\rm ac}(H)\mathbf1_K(H)\phi_p=0$.  The kernel of this bounded projection
is closed and contains a dense set.
\end{proof}

This theorem removes both the growing matrix dimension and the shrinking
constant $\kappa_{\beta,n}$.  At every physical stage only one current
radiation vector must receive a strict contraction.  All earlier probes and
all locked witnesses form a finite preservation list.

\section{The exact compact-output stability estimate}

There is a subtle orientation issue in passing from a cell estimate to an
inverse Jost column.  It can be isolated completely.

\begin{lemma}[Residual control on the ideal inverse output]
\label{lem:compact-output-stability}
Let $D_0=a\Id+zbH$, where $H$ is unitary and $z\in\T$, and let $D$ be
invertible.  For a vector $x$, put
\[
 y_0=D_0^{-1}x,\qquad y=D^{-1}x,
 \qquad M=\|D^{-1}\|.
\]
If
\begin{equation}
 \|(D-D_0)y_0\|\leq\eta,
 \label{eq:residual-control}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{align}
 \|y-y_0\|&\leq M\eta,                              \label{eq:inverse-output-error}\\
 \bigl|\|y\|^2-\|y_0\|^2\bigr|
 &\leq M\eta\bigl(2e^\rho\|x\|+M\eta\bigr).
 \label{eq:density-output-error}
\end{align}
Consequently the error in the $\beta$-fractional density is bounded by the
$\beta$th power of the right side of
\eqref{eq:density-output-error}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $D_0y_0=x=Dy$,
\[
 y-y_0=-D^{-1}(D-D_0)y_0.
\]
This proves~\eqref{eq:inverse-output-error}.  Also
$\|D_0^{-1}\|=(a-b)^{-1}=e^\rho$, and
\[
 |\|y\|^2-\|y_0\|^2|
 \leq(\|y\|+\|y_0\|)\|y-y_0\|.
\]
Use $\|y\|\leq\|y_0\|+M\eta$ and
$|s^\beta-t^\beta|\leq|s-t|^\beta$ for $s,t\geq0$.
\end{proof}

For continuous $H(k)$ and a nonvanishing continuous radiation vector $x(k)$,
the family
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak Y=\left\{
  (a\Id+zbH(k))^{-1}x(k)/\|x(k)\|:
  k\in K,\ z\in\T
 \right\}
 \label{eq:compact-ideal-output-bundle}
\end{equation}
is compact.  Thus the Talbot cell need not be scalar on the whole angular
Hilbert space.  It must control its residual uniformly on
$\mathfrak Y$, together with a finite-dimensional safety space large enough
to absorb the residual.  This is the correct noncircular target.

\begin{lemma}[Reciprocal reflection gap controls the inverse]
\label{lem:reflection-gap}
Let $A,B$ satisfy $A^*A-B^*B=\Id$, assume $A$ is invertible, and put
$C=BA^{-1}$.  If $\|C\|\leq t<1$, then, for every unitary $H$ and every
$z\in\T$,
\begin{equation}
 \|(A+zHB)^{-1}\|\leq\frac1{1-t}.
 \label{eq:reflection-gap-inverse}
\end{equation}
For the localized hyperbolic barrier
\[
 A=\Id+(\cosh\rho-1)P,\qquad B=(\sinh\rho)P,
\]
one has $C=(\tanh\rho)P$.  Reflectionless unitary converters on either side
do not change $\|C\|$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $A^*A\geq\Id$, $\|A^{-1}\|\leq1$.  The factorization
\[
 A+zHB=(\Id+zHC)A
\]
and the Neumann series give~\eqref{eq:reflection-gap-inverse}.  The localized
formula is immediate, and unitary pre- and post-factors preserve singular
values of the reflection contraction.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A moving two-real-direction squeeze]
\label{lem:two-real-direction-squeeze}
Let $H$ be a symmetric unitary on a complex Hilbert space, let
$Q=H\mathcal C$, where $\mathcal Cx=\bar x$, and fix a unit vector $u$.
Put
\begin{equation}
 S_u=|u\rangle\langle u|+|Qu\rangle\langle Qu|.
 \label{eq:two-real-generator}
\end{equation}
For $\rho>0$ define
\begin{equation}
 A_u=\cosh(\rho S_u),\qquad
 B_u=H^*\sinh(\rho S_u).
 \label{eq:two-real-pair}
\end{equation}
Then $(A_u,B_u)$ is reciprocal:
\begin{equation}
 A_u^*A_u-B_u^*B_u=\Id,
 \qquad
 A_u^*\overline{B_u}=B_u^*\overline{A_u}.
 \label{eq:two-real-reciprocity}
\end{equation}
Moreover
\begin{equation}
 A_u+zHB_u
 =\cosh(\rho S_u)+z\sinh(\rho S_u),
 \qquad z\in\T.
 \label{eq:two-real-denominator}
\end{equation}
For every $0<\beta<1$ there is a number
$q^{(2)}_{\beta,\rho}<1$, depending only on $\beta$ and $\rho$, such that
\begin{equation}
 \int_\T
 \left\|(A_u+zHB_u)^{-1}u\right\|^{2\beta}\dd m(z)
 \leq q^{(2)}_{\beta,\rho}.
 \label{eq:two-real-contraction}
\end{equation}
The assertion is dimension free.  The generator $S_u$ has rank at most two
and depends norm-continuously on $(H,u)$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $H$ is symmetric and unitary, $Q$ is a conjugation.  The operator
$S_u$ is positive, has rank at most two, and satisfies $QS_uQ=S_u$.
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 H\overline{S_u}=S_uH.
 \label{eq:two-real-commutation}
\end{equation}
Functional calculus and~\eqref{eq:two-real-commutation} give
\eqref{eq:two-real-reciprocity}; the first identity is simply
$\cosh^2(\rho S_u)-\sinh^2(\rho S_u)=\Id$.  They also give
\eqref{eq:two-real-denominator}.

Let $r=|\langle u,Qu\rangle|\in[0,1]$ and set
\begin{equation}
 g_{\beta,\rho}(s)=\int_\T
  |\cosh(\rho s)+z\sinh(\rho s)|^{-2\beta}\dd m(z),
 \qquad 0\leq s\leq2.
 \label{eq:scalar-two-real-gap}
\end{equation}
The spectral measure of $S_u$ at $u$ has atoms at $1+r$ and $1-r$ with
weights $w_+=(1+r)/2$ and $w_-=(1-r)/2$, respectively.  Put
$t_\pm=\tanh(\rho(1\pm r))$ and
$P_t(z)=(1-t^2)/|1-tz|^2$.  The squared norm inside
\eqref{eq:two-real-contraction} is
\begin{equation}
 s_r(z)=w_+P_{-t_+}(z)+w_-P_{-t_-}(z).
 \label{eq:two-real-poisson-mixture}
\end{equation}
Consequently the left side of~\eqref{eq:two-real-contraction} is
\begin{equation}
 G_{\beta,\rho}(r)=\int_\T s_r(z)^\beta\dd m(z).
 \label{eq:two-real-gap-function}
\end{equation}
The scalar Hardy identity gives $\int_\T s_r\dd m=1$.  The first nonconstant
Fourier coefficient of~\eqref{eq:two-real-poisson-mixture} is
$-(w_+t_++w_-t_-)$, which is strictly negative for every $r\in[0,1]$.
Hence $s_r$ is nonconstant and strict concavity gives
$G_{\beta,\rho}(r)<1$.  The integrand is jointly continuous in $(r,z)$,
including at $r=1$, so $G_{\beta,\rho}$ is continuous on $[0,1]$.  Thus
\[
 q^{(2)}_{\beta,\rho}
 :=\max_{0\leq r\leq1}G_{\beta,\rho}(r)<1.
\]
Finally~\eqref{eq:two-real-generator} proves the asserted norm continuity.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why this bypasses the root-of-unity obstruction]
For a physical Jost matrix,
$H=J^{-1}\overline J$ satisfies $H\overline H=\Id$; together with unitarity
this gives $H^T=H$.  Lemma~\ref{lem:two-real-direction-squeeze} therefore
applies to every current scalar radiation vector.  It does not squeeze a
preassigned growing angular block.  It moves a reciprocal rank-two squeeze
with the target vector, and so the root-of-unity example in
Proposition~\ref{prop:no-dim-free} is irrelevant to this scalar dense-probe
mechanism.

At the distinguished phase $z=1$, the sharper projection version is also
useful.  If $P$ projects onto the $Q$-invariant space
$\operatorname{span}_{\C}\{u,Qu\}$ and
\[
 A=\Id+(\cosh\rho-1)P,\qquad B=(\sinh\rho)H^*P,
\]
then $A+HB=\Id+(e^\rho-1)P$.  Thus the target is multiplied by
$e^{-\rho}$ and the reciprocal phase is unchanged:
\[
 (A+HB)^{-1}H\,\overline{(A+HB)}=H.
\]
This exact repeatability is hidden by a scalar full-block ansatz.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Physical reciprocal Poisson identity]
\label{lem:physical-hardy}
Let $A,B\in\mathbb C^{n\times n}$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
 A^*A-B^*B=\Id,
 \label{eq:physical-flux-identity}
\end{equation}
let $H\in\U(n)$, and put
\[
 F_z=(A+zHB)^{-1},\qquad z\in\T.
\]
Then $A$ is invertible, $F_z$ is invertible for every $z\in\T$, and
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\T}F_z^*F_z\,\dd m(z)=\Id.
 \label{eq:physical-hardy-identity}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for every matrix $X$ and every $0<\beta<1$,
\begin{align}
 \int_{\T}X^*F_z^*F_zX\,\dd m(z)&=X^*X,
 \label{eq:physical-matrix-martingale}\\
 \int_{\T}\tr\!\left[(X^*F_z^*F_zX)^\beta\right]\dd m(z)
 &\leq\tr\!\left[(X^*X)^\beta\right].
 \label{eq:physical-fractional-nonexpansion}
\end{align}
The same conclusion for a scalar probe is
\[
 \int_{\T}\|F_zx\|^{2\beta}\dd m(z)\leq\|x\|^{2\beta}.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Equation~\eqref{eq:physical-flux-identity} gives $A^*A\succeq\Id$, so
$A$ is invertible.  Set $C=BA^{-1}$ and $T=-HC$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 C^*C
 =A^{-*}B^*BA^{-1}
 =\Id-A^{-*}A^{-1},
 \qquad \|C\|<1,
 \label{eq:physical-defect-identity}
\end{equation}
and
\[
 F_z=A^{-1}(\Id-zT)^{-1}.
\]
Therefore
\begin{align*}
 F_z^*F_z
 &=(\Id-\bar zT^*)^{-1}
   (\Id-T^*T)(\Id-zT)^{-1}.
\end{align*}
Termwise integration of the norm-convergent Neumann series gives
\begin{align*}
 \int_{\T}F_z^*F_z\dd m(z)
 &=\sum_{q\geq0}T^{*q}(\Id-T^*T)T^q\\
 &=\Id-\lim_{q\to\infty}T^{*(q+1)}T^{q+1}=\Id.
\end{align*}
This proves~\eqref{eq:physical-hardy-identity} and
\eqref{eq:physical-matrix-martingale}.  Operator concavity of
$Y\mapsto Y^\beta$, followed by the trace, proves
\eqref{eq:physical-fractional-nonexpansion}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why this is stronger than the ideal Hardy identity]
\label{rem:physical-hardy-meaning}
No scalarity or commutativity of $A$, $B$, and $H$ is used.  Thus an outer
physical cell may behave arbitrarily away from its tuning window: after a
rapid scalar collar phase is averaged, every protected quadratic density is
preserved and every protected fractional moment is nonexpansive.  In a finite
reachable block, deterministic large collar frequencies realize these phase
means by the same Fourier approximation and Riemann--Lebesgue argument as
Lemma~\ref{lem:one-step-placement}.  Proposition
~\ref{prop:explicit-buffer-stability} then transfers the assertion to the
 specified finite list of full outgoing probe densities, with a prescribed
 summable error.  This is the preservation input used in Proposition
 ~\ref{prop:physical-grouped-one-window}; by itself it does not supply a strict
 contraction away from the cell's tuning window.
\end{remark}

Thus a cell with scattering-norm error $\varepsilon<1-\tanh\rho$ has an
inverse bound depending on $\rho$ and $\varepsilon$, but not on its radial
location or on an evanescent transfer condition number.  Combining this with
Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-output-stability} shows that it is enough to make the
direct residual $o(1-\tanh\rho)$ on the finite safety block.

\begin{remark}[Why a global inverse bound may not be assumed]
At a fixed radial shell, sufficiently high angular momenta are evanescent.
Their inward/outward transfer coefficients can be exponentially ill
conditioned even though the physical scattering matrix is unitary.  Hence a
formal use of Lemma~\ref{lem:compact-output-stability} with a full-space
constant $M$ is invalid.  One must first eliminate the evanescent tail by an
outgoing Schur complement and apply the lemma on a finite reachable block.
This is the precise place where the earlier scale ledger was incomplete.
\end{remark}

The required elimination is stable at the resolvent level.  This avoids every
raw evanescent transfer condition number.

\begin{lemma}[Schur removal measured from the full outgoing problem]
\label{lem:schur-stability}
Let
\[
 \mathcal A=
 \begin{pmatrix}A&B^*\\ B&C\end{pmatrix}
\]
be an invertible closed block operator, with $C$ invertible, and put
$\Sigma=B^*C^{-1}B$ and $S=A-\Sigma$.  If
$\|\mathcal A^{-1}\|\leq M_0$ and $M_0\|\Sigma\|<1$, then $A$ is invertible
and
\begin{equation}
 \|A^{-1}-S^{-1}\|
 \leq\frac{M_0^2\|\Sigma\|}{1-M_0\|\Sigma\|}.
 \label{eq:schur-stability}
\end{equation}
In particular, the right side is at most $2M_0^2\|\Sigma\|$ when
$M_0\|\Sigma\|\leq1/2$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The upper-left block of $\mathcal A^{-1}$ is $S^{-1}$, so
$\|S^{-1}\|\leq M_0$.  Since $A=S+\Sigma$,
\[
 A^{-1}=(\Id+S^{-1}\Sigma)^{-1}S^{-1}.
\]
The Neumann bound and the resolvent identity give
\eqref{eq:schur-stability}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Explicit outgoing buffer stability]
\label{prop:explicit-buffer-stability}
Let $U$ be a fixed bounded real potential supported in $1\leq r\leq R$,
let $K=[k_0,k_1]\Subset(0,\infty)$, and let $\mathcal A_U(k)$ be the
Dirichlet problem on $(1,R)$ with the exact free outgoing
Dirichlet-to-Neumann condition at $R$.  Then
\begin{equation}
 M_{U,K}:=\sup_{k\in K}\|\mathcal A_U(k)^{-1}\|<\infty.
 \label{eq:full-outgoing-bound}
\end{equation}
If $P_J$ contains the angular degrees at most $J$ and
\[
 g_{J,R}=\frac{(J+1)(J+2)}{R^2}-\|U\|_\infty-k_1^2>0,
\]
then, once $M_{U,K}\|U\|_\infty^2/g_{J,R}\leq1/2$, the full and
$P_J$-truncated outgoing resolvents, compressed to $P_J$, differ by at most
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{k\in K}
 \|P_J\mathcal A_U(k)^{-1}P_J-\mathcal A_{U,J}(k)^{-1}\|
 \leq
 2M_{U,K}^2\frac{\|U\|_\infty^2}{g_{J,R}}.
 \label{eq:explicit-buffer-resolvent}
\end{equation}
The same bound, multiplied by $\|f\|^2/\pi$, controls the difference of the
spectral densities of every source $f$ lying in the retained block.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The outgoing boundary problem is elliptic and Fredholm.  A real-axis
homogeneous solution has zero outgoing flux by Green's identity; positivity
of the channel flux forces its far-field amplitude to vanish, and unique
continuation gives the zero solution.  Thus the problem is invertible.
Continuous dependence on $k\in K$ proves~\eqref{eq:full-outgoing-bound}.

On the omitted sector, centrifugal coercivity gives
$\|C^{-1}\|\leq g_{J,R}^{-1}$, while $\|B\|\leq\|U\|_\infty$.  Hence
$\|\Sigma\|\leq\|U\|_\infty^2/g_{J,R}$.  Apply
Lemma~\ref{lem:schur-stability}.  Finally, the density is the imaginary part
of the outgoing resolvent quadratic form divided by $\pi$.
\end{proof}

\section{Full-block Talbot concentration: what actually changes}

Let
\[
 A_3=-\Delta_{\mathbb S^2},
\]
and let $h_L(\theta)=h(L\theta)$ be a fast zonal factor.  For an arbitrary
smooth envelope $f(\theta,\varphi)$ the invariant product identity is
\begin{equation}
 A_3(h_Lf)=(A_3h_L)f-2\nabla h_L\cdot\nabla f+h_LA_3f.
 \label{eq:product-laplacian}
\end{equation}
The first term is exactly the term in the existing zonal Talbot proof.  On a
fixed finite-dimensional $\mathcal K\subset C^\infty(\mathbb S^2)$, the two
new terms obey
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{\substack{f\in\mathcal K\\\|f\|_2=1}}
 \left(
  \|\nabla h_L\cdot\nabla f\|_2+\|h_LA_3f\|_2
 \right)=O_{\mathcal K,h}(L).
 \label{eq:envelope-error}
\end{equation}
After a Talbot flight of length $O(L^{-2})$, this is $O(L^{-1})$.  The polar
term already present in $A_3h_L$ has the known bound
$O(L\sqrt{\log L})$, hence contributes
$O(\sqrt{\log L}/L)$.  No new $\varphi$-singularity appears in
\eqref{eq:product-laplacian}.

\begin{proposition}[Finite-block extension of the Talbot lemma]
\label{prop:full-block-talbot}
The scalar polar estimate used in the zonal Talbot proposition holds by
splitting the two polar caps at scale $L^{-1}$ and integrating
$\cot^2\theta\sin\theta$ on their complement.  That proposition and its
reciprocal inverse therefore hold, with
the same $O(\sqrt{\log L}/L)$ rate, for every fixed finite-dimensional
conjugation-invariant
$\mathcal K\subset C^\infty(\mathbb S^2)$, without a zonality assumption.
Consequently the Riesz concentration, small-support cutoff, and reverse
refocusing estimates also hold uniformly on the unit sphere of
$\mathcal K$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Use~\eqref{eq:product-laplacian} in the same Duhamel comparison as in the
zonal proof.  Equation~\eqref{eq:envelope-error} is uniform because all
smooth norms are equivalent on a fixed finite-dimensional space.  The
leading fast term and the polar remainder are unchanged.  This proves the
one-factor estimate.  For a product, choose the frequencies successively;
after finitely many previous finite-band factors the set of possible
envelopes is still finite dimensional.  The uniform Riemann--Lebesgue and
Chernoff steps used for the tilted Riesz density apply to the compact unit
sphere of this enlarged space.  Reverse the factors to obtain refocusing.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[A fixed height realizes arbitrarily strong reciprocal barriers]
\label{lem:bounded-reflector}
Fix $k_\star>0$.  There are a number $B<\infty$ and a smooth real elementary
one-dimensional cell such that, using finitely many translates of this cell
and free phase intervals, one can realize at $k_\star$ every Cartan boost
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}\cosh\rho&\sinh\rho\\
                 \sinh\rho&\cosh\rho\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad \rho>0,
\]
up to arbitrary prescribed error, while the resulting potential is bounded
by $B$.  Its length and radial $L^2$ cost are finite numbers
$\ell_\rho,C_\rho$ depending on $\rho$, and its reflection contraction is at
most $\tanh(\rho+C_0)$ for one fixed $C_0$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The transfer matrix of a nonreflectionless smooth real bump belongs to
$\mathrm{SU}(1,1)$ and has a Cartan decomposition
$R_\alpha B_{\rho_0}R_\gamma$ with $\rho_0>0$.  Free intervals realize the
rotations $R_\theta$.  Cancel the end rotations between successive copies;
$n$ aligned copies give $B_{n\rho_0}$.  A final bump whose coupling is varied
from zero to its fixed value has a continuous Cartan parameter joining zero
to $\rho_0$, and therefore supplies every remainder in $[0,\rho_0]$ after
its end rotations are compensated.  No copy exceeds the fixed bump height.
Cartan parameters are
subadditive under multiplication, so every partially switched or transition
cell has parameter at most $\rho+C_0$, which proves the reflection bound.
Finitely many bounded compact cells have finite length and $L^2$ cost.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[The physical scale inequalities are compatible]
\label{lem:scale-compatibility}
Fix a finite Riesz depth $m$, hyperbolic angle $\rho$, largest integer
frequency ratio $Q$, and accuracy $0<\varepsilon<1$.  Choose the finitely
many weak-slab lengths equal to a common $T$; let $\Lambda$ be the common
carrier, $D=Q\Lambda$ the
largest physical angular frequency, $R$ the cell radius, $J$ the active
outgoing cutoff, and $L$ the Galerkin cutoff.  Let
$\ell_\rho<\infty$ be the radial length of any fixed bounded one-dimensional
realization of the desired hyperbolic reflector.  The scales can be chosen
successively so that
\begin{align}
 T^{-1}&<\varepsilon,
 &\frac{D^2T}{R^2}+\frac TR&<\varepsilon,
 &\frac{D^2(1+\ell_\rho)}{R^2}&<\varepsilon,
 \label{eq:scale-mask-reflector}\\
 \frac{\sqrt{\log\Lambda}}{\Lambda}&<\varepsilon,
 &\frac R{\Lambda^2}&<\varepsilon,
 &\frac RJ+\frac JL&<\varepsilon.
 \label{eq:scale-talbot-buffer}
\end{align}
The weak-mask $X_3$ cost can simultaneously be made smaller than
$\varepsilon$.  None of these choices changes the reflector cost
$C_\rho e^{-cm}$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
First choose all weak-slab lengths so large that the sum of their
$T^{-1}\|w\|_2^2$ costs and their one-dimensional errors is below
$\varepsilon$.  Keeping this finite $T$, choose $\Lambda$ so large that
\[
 Q\Lambda\sqrt{T/\varepsilon}
 +T/\varepsilon
 +Q\Lambda\sqrt{(1+\ell_\rho)/\varepsilon}
 \ll \varepsilon\Lambda^2
\]
and the polar error in~\eqref{eq:scale-talbot-buffer} is below
$\varepsilon$.  There is then an $R$ strictly between the left side and
$\varepsilon\Lambda^2$.  This gives
\eqref{eq:scale-mask-reflector} and the middle inequality in
\eqref{eq:scale-talbot-buffer}.  Finally choose $J/R$ and then $L/J$
arbitrarily large.  The carrier, radii, and cutoffs do not enter the charged
cost, proving the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Window count does not consume the $X_3$ budget]
\label{lem:window-cost}
Fix $\rho$ and let $A_\rho<\infty$ be any accuracy amplification required by
Lemmas~\ref{lem:compact-output-stability} and~\ref{lem:reflection-gap}.
Suppose a depth-$m$ cell has chromatic error
$Cm|k/k_\star-1|+\varepsilon$ and reflector cost
$C_\rho e^{-cm}$.  For every generation budget $\zeta>0$, one can choose
$m$, a finite cover of $K$ by tuning windows, and the physical errors of the
cells so that their total integrated $\beta$-fractional density error and
their total $X_3$ cost are both at most $\zeta$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Partition $K$ into windows of length at most
\[
 h_m=\frac1{C A_\rho m}
      \left(\frac{\zeta}{4|K|}\right)^{1/\beta}.
\]
There are at most
$N_m\leq C_KA_\rho m\zeta^{-1/\beta}$ windows.  On their union the integrated
fractional error caused by the chromatic term is bounded by
\[
 \sum_i\int_{I_i}(A_\rho Cm h_m)^\beta\dd k
 \leq\zeta/4.
\]
Choose the nonchromatic residual in every target window no larger than
$Cm h_m$; it contributes the same bound.  Since
$N_mC_\rho e^{-cm}\to0$, choose $m$ so large that the total reflector cost is
at most $\zeta/2$.  All errors which act on the whole of $K$, including
phase-placement and protected-test errors, are instead allocated a summable
budget $O(\zeta/N_m)$ per cell.  Lemma
~\ref{lem:scale-compatibility} realizes those finite requests.  Further
finite preservation tests only subdivide this allocation.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Local contraction without coverage is insufficient]
\label{prop:coverage-obstruction}
Let $K$ have positive length and let $(h_j)$ be positive with
$\sum_jh_j<|K|$.  For every adaptive choice of intervals
$I_j\subset K$ with $|I_j|\leq h_j$, there are nonnegative densities $w_j$
such that the $j$th step contracts $w_{j-1}^\beta$ by a fixed factor on
$I_j$, is exactly nonexpansive off $I_j$, and may be assigned arbitrarily
small formal cost, but
\[
 \inf_j\int_Kw_j^\beta\dd k>0.
\]
Consequently, one-window cells plus a summable cost ledger do not imply the
global hypothesis~\eqref{eq:dovetail-contract}.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $w_0=1$ and define
\[
 w_j(k)=
 \begin{cases}
  2^{-1/\beta}w_{j-1}(k),&k\in I_j,\\
  w_{j-1}(k),&k\notin I_j.
 \end{cases}
\]
Every stated local property holds.  But
$E=K\setminus\bigcup_jI_j$ has
$|E|\geq|K|-\sum_jh_j>0$, and $w_j=1$ on $E$ for every $j$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Singular phases have no uniform chromatic modulus]
\label{prop:no-uniform-chromatic-modulus}
Let $H_N$ be diagonal with the $N$th roots of unity and let $u_N$ give each
eigenvalue weight $1/N$.  Put $t=\tanh\rho$ and
\[
 Q_{\beta,N}(t)=\int_\T
 \|(\cosh\rho\,\Id+z\sinh\rho\,H_N)^{-*}u_N\|^{2\beta}\dd m(z).
\]
For every fixed $q<1$ there is $c_{\beta,q}>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
 Q_{\beta,N}(t)\leq q
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \rho\geq\tfrac12\log N-c_{\beta,q},
 \qquad
 \frac1{1-t}\geq c_{\beta,q}N.
 \label{eq:chromatic-modulus-loss}
\end{equation}
Thus singularity of the reciprocal phase, even for a finite-rank
perturbation of a pure-point unitary, supplies no dimension-free lower bound
on the physical tuning-window width.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The root-of-unity average collapses the Poisson series exactly:
\[
 \|(\cosh\rho\,\Id+z\sinh\rho\,H_N)^{-*}u_N\|^2
 =1+2\sum_{j\geq1}(-t)^{jN}\cos(jN\arg z).
\]
Up to a rotation and the measure-preserving map $z\mapsto z^N$, this is the
Poisson kernel $P_{t^N}$.  Hence
\[
 Q_{\beta,N}(t)=\int_\T P_{t^N}(z)^\beta\dd m(z).
\]
The last integral is continuous in $r=t^N$ and equals one at $r=0$.
Therefore $Q_{\beta,N}(t)\leq q<1$ forces $t^N\geq r_{\beta,q}>0$.
It follows that
$1-t\leq-\log t\leq-N^{-1}\log r_{\beta,q}$, proving the second assertion;
$e^{2\rho}=(1+t)/(1-t)$ proves the first.  The example acts on a finite
subspace and can be extended by any fixed pure-point diagonal unitary on its
orthogonal complement, changing that unitary by finite rank.
\end{proof}

\section{Synchronization before contraction: the grouped Lemma B}

The one-step root-of-unity obstruction does not survive a finite reciprocal
burn-in.  The reason is that the same scalar cells which may initially have
almost no fractional-moment gap synchronize every finite reciprocal phase.
After synchronization, a fixed number of further cells contracts every
vector in the block.  This is the missing grouping which is absent from the
one-step formulation of Lemma B.

Put
\begin{equation}
 \phi_z(h)=\frac{ah+\bar z b}{a+zbh},
 \qquad h,z\in\T,
 \label{eq:mobius-scalar-action}
\end{equation}
where $a=\cosh\rho$ and $b=\sinh\rho$.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform reciprocal synchronization]
\label{lem:uniform-reciprocal-synchronization}
There are $0<\alpha<1$ and $0<\sigma<1$, depending only on $\rho$, such that
\begin{equation}
 \int_\T |\phi_z(h)-\phi_z(\lambda)|^\alpha\dd m(z)
 \leq \sigma |h-\lambda|^\alpha,
 \qquad h,\lambda\in\T.
 \label{eq:uniform-pair-synchronization}
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $H_0\in\U(n)$ and
$H_j=\phi_{z_j}(H_{j-1})$ for independent Haar phases, then for every
$0<\delta<2$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P\{\operatorname{diam}\sigma(H_M)>\delta\}
 \leq n^2(2/\delta)^\alpha\sigma^M.
 \label{eq:spectral-synchronization-probability}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The exact chord identity is
\begin{equation}
 \frac{|\phi_z(h)-\phi_z(\lambda)|}{|h-\lambda|}
 =\frac1{|a+zbh|\,|a+zb\lambda|}.
 \label{eq:mobius-chord-ratio}
\end{equation}
Let
\[
 Y_z=-\log|a+zbh|-\log|a+zb\lambda|.
\]
Since $a-b=e^{-\rho}$ and $a+b=e^\rho$, one has
$|Y_z|\leq2\rho$.  Jensen's formula gives, uniformly in
$h,\lambda\in\T$,
\[
 \int_\T Y_z\dd m(z)=-2\log a<0.
\]
Hoeffding's lemma therefore yields
\begin{equation}
 \int_\T e^{\alpha Y_z}\dd m(z)
 \leq \exp\!\left(-2\alpha\log a+2\alpha^2\rho^2\right).
 \label{eq:pair-hoeffding}
\end{equation}
Choose $0<\alpha<\min\{1,(\log a)/\rho^2\}$ and call the right side
$\sigma<1$.  This proves~\eqref{eq:uniform-pair-synchronization}.

The eigenvalues of $H_j$ are obtained by applying the same scalar map
\eqref{eq:mobius-scalar-action} to the eigenvalues of $H_{j-1}$.  Iterate
\eqref{eq:uniform-pair-synchronization}, apply Markov's inequality to every
pair, and take the union bound over at most $n^2$ ordered pairs.  This proves
\eqref{eq:spectral-synchronization-probability}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Dimension-free grouped contraction]
\label{thm:grouped-ideal-contraction}
Fix $\rho>0$.  There are $0<\beta<1$, an integer $R\geq1$, and a constant
$q_\star<1$, depending only on $\rho$, with the following property.  For
every $n\geq1$ there is an integer
\begin{equation}
 M(n)\leq C_\rho\log(n+1)+C_\rho
 \label{eq:burnin-log-dimension}
\end{equation}
such that, for every $H_0\in\U(n)$ and every matrix $X_0$, the ideal scalar
recursion
\begin{align}
 F_j&=(a\Id+z_jbH_{j-1})^{-1},
 &X_j&=F_jX_{j-1},                                      \label{eq:grouped-X-recursion}\\
 H_j&=(a\Id+z_jbH_{j-1})^{-1}
       (aH_{j-1}+\bar z_jb\Id)                          \label{eq:grouped-H-recursion}
\end{align}
satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\T^{M(n)+R}}
  \tr\!\left[(X_{M(n)+R}^*X_{M(n)+R})^\beta\right]
  \dd m^{M(n)+R}
 \leq q_\star\tr\!\left[(X_0^*X_0)^\beta\right].
 \label{eq:grouped-dimension-free-contraction}
\end{equation}
The same estimate holds, with the same $q_\star$, for
$\sum_{p=1}^N\|x_{p,M(n)+R}\|^{2\beta}$ starting from any finite family of
vectors and evolving each one by the same factors $F_j$.
Thus one \emph{group} of scalar full-block cells has a fixed contraction
factor even though no single cell does.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Take $\alpha,\sigma$ from Lemma
~\ref{lem:uniform-reciprocal-synchronization}.  Choose $\beta>0$ so small
that
\begin{equation}
 \theta:=\sigma e^{2\beta\rho}<1.
 \label{eq:bad-event-exponent}
\end{equation}
For
\[
 q_{\beta,\rho}=\int_\T|a+zb|^{-2\beta}\dd m(z)<1,
\]
choose $\delta_0>0$ so small that
\begin{equation}
 q_c:=(1-be^\rho\delta_0)^{-2\beta}q_{\beta,\rho}<1.
 \label{eq:synchronized-one-step-factor}
\end{equation}
Then choose $R$ with $q_c^R\leq1/4$ and put
$\delta=\delta_0e^{-2\rho R}$.

Let $\mathcal G$ be the event
$\operatorname{diam}\sigma(H_M)\leq\delta$.  On $\mathcal G$, choose one
eigenvalue $\lambda$ of $H_M$.  Normality gives
$\|H_M-\lambda\Id\|\leq\delta$.  The chord identity shows that one further
M\"obius step can enlarge spectral diameter by at most $e^{2\rho}$.
Therefore throughout the next $R$ steps the current phase is within
$\delta_0$ of a scalar unitary.  The resolvent factorization
\begin{equation}
 (a\Id+zbH)^{-1}
 =(a+zb\lambda)^{-1}
 \left[\Id+\frac{zb}{a+zb\lambda}(H-\lambda\Id)\right]^{-1}
 \label{eq:synchronized-resolvent-factorization}
\end{equation}
and~\eqref{eq:synchronized-one-step-factor} imply, conditionally at each of
these $R$ steps,
\begin{equation}
 \int_\T\tr[(X^*F_z^*F_zX)^\beta]\dd m(z)
 \leq q_c\tr[(X^*X)^\beta].
 \label{eq:good-event-contraction}
\end{equation}

During the first $M$ steps, Lemma~\ref{lem:physical-hardy} gives exact
phase-mean nonexpansion.  Pointwise, however,
$\|F_j\|\leq(a-b)^{-1}=e^\rho$, so
\begin{equation}
 \tr[(X_M^*X_M)^\beta]
 \leq e^{2\beta\rho M}\tr[(X_0^*X_0)^\beta].
 \label{eq:burnin-pointwise-growth}
\end{equation}
Combining~\eqref{eq:spectral-synchronization-probability},
\eqref{eq:bad-event-exponent}, and~\eqref{eq:burnin-pointwise-growth} gives
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\mathcal G^c}\tr[(X_M^*X_M)^\beta]\dd m^M
 \leq n^2(2/\delta)^\alpha\theta^M
       \tr[(X_0^*X_0)^\beta].
 \label{eq:bad-event-moment}
\end{equation}
Choose $M=M(n)$ so that the coefficient on the right is at most $1/4$.
This has the logarithmic bound~\eqref{eq:burnin-log-dimension}.  On
$\mathcal G$, iterate~\eqref{eq:good-event-contraction}; on
$\mathcal G^c$, use phase-mean nonexpansion for the last $R$ cells.  The two
contributions are at most $1/4$ each.  Hence
\eqref{eq:grouped-dimension-free-contraction} holds with $q_\star=1/2$.
For a finite vector family, apply the scalar form of Lemma
~\ref{lem:physical-hardy} during burn-in and sum the good-event resolvent
bound and the bad-event pointwise bound over $p$.  No constant depends on the
number of vectors.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-height grouped contraction for singular bundles]
\label{thm:singular-bundle-grouped-contraction}
Fix $\rho>0$ and $0<\beta<1$.  Let $\mathfrak M$ be a weakly compact family
of probability measures on $\T$, every member of which is singular with
respect to Haar measure.  For every $0<q<1$ there is an integer
$M=M(\rho,\beta,\mathfrak M,q)$ such that the following holds.

For $z\in\T$ put
\begin{equation}
 S(z)=
 \begin{pmatrix}a&\bar z b\\zb&a\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad
 S(z_M)\cdots S(z_1)=
 \begin{pmatrix}A_M&\overline{B_M}\\B_M&\overline{A_M}\end{pmatrix}.
 \label{eq:su11-random-product}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{\nu\in\mathfrak M}
 \int_{\T^M}
 \left(
  \int_\T |B_Mh+\overline{A_M}|^{-2}\dd\nu(h)
 \right)^\beta\dd m^M
 \leq q.
 \label{eq:singular-bundle-product-contraction}
\end{equation}
Consequently, if $H$ is unitary and the normalized spectral measures of a
finite vector family $x_1,\ldots,x_N$ belong to $\mathfrak M$, then the ideal
recursion~\eqref{eq:grouped-X-recursion}--\eqref{eq:grouped-H-recursion}
satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \int_{\T^M}\sum_{p=1}^N\|x_{p,M}\|^{2\beta}\dd m^M
 \leq q\sum_{p=1}^N\|x_p\|^{2\beta}.
 \label{eq:singular-vector-bundle-contraction}
\end{equation}
The height $\rho$ is fixed; only the finite group length depends on the
singular bundle.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The matrices in~\eqref{eq:su11-random-product} lie in $\mathrm{SU}(1,1)$, so
\begin{equation}
 |A_M|^2-|B_M|^2=1.
 \label{eq:su11-hyperbolic-identity}
\end{equation}
Let $r_M=|B_M/A_M|$.  Conjugation by
$K_\vartheta=\operatorname{diag}(e^{i\vartheta/2},e^{-i\vartheta/2})$
sends $S(z)$ to $S(ze^{-i\vartheta})$.  Product Haar measure is invariant
under this simultaneous rotation, while conjugation fixes $A_M$ and rotates
$B_M$.  Hence, conditionally on $r_M$, the argument of
$-B_M/\overline{A_M}$ is Haar-uniform.  By
\eqref{eq:su11-hyperbolic-identity}, averaging first over that argument gives
the exact identity
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb E
 \left(
  \int_\T |B_Mh+\overline{A_M}|^{-2}\dd\nu(h)
 \right)^\beta
 =\mathbb E\,Q_{r_M}(\nu),
 \label{eq:random-product-poisson-identity}
\end{equation}
where $Q_r(\nu)=\int_\T(P_r*\nu)^\beta\dd m$ is the quantity used in the
proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:strong-barrier}.

It remains to show that $r_M$ approaches one.  With
$L_j=\log|A_j|$ and the product taken in the order in
\eqref{eq:su11-random-product},
\[
 L_j-L_{j-1}
 =\log\left|a+\bar z_jb\frac{B_{j-1}}{A_{j-1}}\right|.
\]
Jensen's formula and $|B_{j-1}/A_{j-1}|<1$ give
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb E(L_j-L_{j-1}\mid z_1,\ldots,z_{j-1})=\log a>0,
 \label{eq:positive-radial-drift}
\end{equation}
and every increment lies in $[-\rho,\rho]$.  Azuma--Hoeffding therefore gives
\begin{equation}
 \mathbb P\left\{L_M<\tfrac12M\log a\right\}
 \leq
 \exp\left[-\frac{M(\log a)^2}{8\rho^2}\right].
 \label{eq:effective-height-concentration}
\end{equation}
On the complementary event,
\begin{equation}
 r_M=\sqrt{1-|A_M|^{-2}}
 \geq\sqrt{1-a^{-M}}.
 \label{eq:effective-poisson-radius}
\end{equation}

The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:strong-barrier} shows that
$Q_r(\nu)\downarrow0$ as $r\uparrow1$ for every singular $\nu$, uniformly on
the compact set $\mathfrak M$ by monotonicity, continuity, and Dini's theorem.
Also $0\leq Q_r(\nu)\leq1$.  Choose $r_0<1$ so that
$\sup_{\nu\in\mathfrak M}Q_{r_0}(\nu)\leq q/2$, and then choose $M$ so large
that~\eqref{eq:effective-height-concentration} is at most $q/2$ and the
right side of~\eqref{eq:effective-poisson-radius} is at least $r_0$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:random-product-poisson-identity} proves
\eqref{eq:singular-bundle-product-contraction}.

Finally, direct multiplication of the scalar M\"obius factors gives
\begin{equation}
 x_{p,M}=(B_MH+\overline{A_M}\Id)^{-1}x_p.
 \label{eq:collapsed-vector-recursion}
\end{equation}
Apply~\eqref{eq:singular-bundle-product-contraction} to each normalized vector
spectral measure, multiply by $\|x_p\|^{2\beta}$, and sum over $p$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Grouped root-of-unity lower bound and the Zeno certificate]
\label{prop:grouped-root-zeno}
Fix $\rho>0$, $0<\beta<1$, and $0<q<1$.  Let $\nu_N$ give mass $1/N$ to
the $N$th roots of unity.  For the product in
\eqref{eq:su11-random-product}, put
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Q_{M,N}=
 \int_{\T^M}
 \left(
  \int_\T |B_Mh+\overline{A_M}|^{-2}\dd\nu_N(h)
 \right)^\beta\dd m^M.
 \label{eq:grouped-root-moment}
\end{equation}
There is $C_{\beta,q,\rho}<\infty$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Q_{M,N}\leq q
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 M\geq\frac{\log N}{2\rho}-C_{\beta,q,\rho}.
 \label{eq:grouped-root-length-lower-bound}
\end{equation}

Consequently singularity alone gives no nonsummable lower bound for the
windows certified by the present Riesz--Talbot estimate.  Indeed, if a group
of $M_j$ depth-$m_j$ cells is certified only through
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{err}_j(k)
 \leq C M_jm_j|k/k_j-1|+\varepsilon_j,
 \label{eq:grouped-certified-chromatic-error}
\end{equation}
then the standard certified half-width at fixed error tolerance $\eta$ is
$h_j=\eta/(CM_jm_j)$.  Taking
$N_j=\lceil e^{j^2}\rceil$ in
\eqref{eq:grouped-root-length-lower-bound} gives
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j\geq1}h_j<\infty
 \qquad(m_j\geq1).
 \label{eq:root-zeno-sum}
\end{equation}
Thus the implication ``singular phase plus one-window contraction implies
global hazard divergence'' is false at the level of the currently proved
black-box estimates.  A proof of Problem
~\ref{prob:physical-grouped-global} must use additional structure of the
actually generated phase bundles or a genuinely broadband cancellation; it
cannot follow from singularity alone.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write $r_M=|B_M/A_M|$.  The Cartan length on $\mathrm{SU}(1,1)$ is
subadditive, so
\begin{equation}
 r_M\leq\tanh(M\rho).
 \label{eq:product-cartan-upper-bound}
\end{equation}
As in the root-of-unity calculation in Proposition
~\ref{prop:no-uniform-chromatic-modulus}, averaging over the conditional
Haar centre of the product gives
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal Q_{M,N}
 =\mathbb E\,Q_\beta(r_M^N),
 \qquad
 Q_\beta(s)=\int_\T P_s(z)^\beta\dd m(z).
 \label{eq:grouped-root-collapse}
\end{equation}
The function $Q_\beta$ decreases continuously from $1$ to $0$.  Hence
\eqref{eq:product-cartan-upper-bound} implies
\[
 \mathcal Q_{M,N}
 \geq Q_\beta\bigl(\tanh(M\rho)^N\bigr).
\]
If the left side is at most $q$, then
$\tanh(M\rho)^N\geq s_{\beta,q}>0$.  Therefore
$1-\tanh(M\rho)\leq C_{\beta,q}/N$.  Since
$1-\tanh x=2/(e^{2x}+1)$, this is
\eqref{eq:grouped-root-length-lower-bound}.  Substituting
$N_j=\lceil e^{j^2}\rceil$ into the stated certified width proves
\eqref{eq:root-zeno-sum}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[A pure-point M\"obius orbit can accumulate on Haar measure]
\label{prop:mobius-orbit-haar-obstruction}
For
\[
 g=\begin{pmatrix}A&\overline B\\B&\overline A\end{pmatrix}
 \in\mathrm{SU}(1,1),
 \qquad |A|^2-|B|^2=1,
\]
define the projective action on probability measures on $\T$ by
\begin{align}
 \Psi_g(h)&=\frac{Ah+\overline B}{Bh+\overline A},
 \label{eq:su11-boundary-action}\\
 \mathcal T_g\nu
 &=
 \frac{(\Psi_g)_*
  \bigl(|Bh+\overline A|^{-2}\nu\bigr)}
 {\displaystyle\int_\T|Bh+\overline A|^{-2}\dd\nu(h)}.
 \label{eq:su11-spectral-measure-action}
\end{align}
There are a pure-point probability measure $\nu$ on $\T$ and elements
$g_n\in\mathrm{SU}(1,1)$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal T_{g_n}\nu\ \longrightarrow\ m
 \quad\hbox{weakly},
 \label{eq:mobius-orbit-to-haar}
\end{equation}
where $m$ is Haar measure.

Consequently, for every fixed group length $M$ and fixed cell height
$\rho>0$,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{g\in\mathrm{SU}(1,1)}
 \int_{\T^M}
 \left(
  \int_\T
  |B_Mh+\overline{A_M}|^{-2}
  \dd(\mathcal T_g\nu)(h)
 \right)^\beta
 \dd m^M
 =1.
 \label{eq:no-uniform-mobius-history-contraction}
\end{equation}
Thus even a single pure-point starting phase need not have a compact
singular M\"obius-history bundle.  The group length in Theorem
~\ref{thm:singular-bundle-grouped-contraction} cannot be made uniform over
all prior ideal scalar histories without an additional restriction.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Write
\[
 K_g(h)=|Bh+\overline A|^{-2}.
\]
Up to a rotation, $K_g$ is a Poisson kernel.  A boost whose attracting
endpoint tends to $p\in\T$ has
\begin{equation}
 K_g(h)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\hbox{uniformly on compact subsets of }\T\setminus\{p\}.
 \label{eq:boost-kernel-off-pole}
\end{equation}
The unnormalized transformations
\[
 \mathcal L_g\nu=(\Psi_g)_*(K_g\nu)
\]
compose according to the group law.  In particular,
$\mathcal T_g$ is invertible with inverse $\mathcal T_{g^{-1}}$, and if
$\sigma=\mathcal T_{g^{-1}}\mu$, then
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal L_g\sigma
 =Z_g(\sigma)\mu,
 \qquad
 Z_g(\sigma)=
 \frac1{\displaystyle\int_\T K_{g^{-1}}\dd\mu}.
 \label{eq:inverse-cluster-normalization}
\end{equation}

Let $\mu_n$ be normalized counting measure on the $N_n$th roots of unity,
where $N_n\to\infty$, and let $\varepsilon_n\downarrow0$.  We construct a
purely atomic mixture
\[
 \nu=a_0\delta_{h_0}+\sum_{n\geq1}a_n\sigma_n,
 \qquad
 a_n>0,\quad \sum_{n\geq0}a_n=1,
 \label{eq:atomic-zoom-mixture}
\]
by induction.  Suppose the first $n-1$ finite clusters have been chosen.
Choose two boundary points which avoid the finite support already present
and the finite support of $\mu_n$.  Along boosts $g$ with these attracting
and repelling endpoints,~\eqref{eq:boost-kernel-off-pole} makes
\[
 \int K_g\,\dd
 \left(a_0\delta_{h_0}+\sum_{j<n}a_j\sigma_j\right)
 \longrightarrow0,
 \]
while~\eqref{eq:inverse-cluster-normalization}, with
$\sigma=\mathcal T_{g^{-1}}\mu_n$, makes $Z_g(\sigma)$ tend to infinity.
After choosing $a_n>0$ small enough to satisfy all previously imposed tail
bounds, take the boost sufficiently long that the past contribution is at
most
\[
 \varepsilon_n a_nZ_{g_n}(\sigma_n),
 \qquad
 \sigma_n=\mathcal T_{g_n^{-1}}\mu_n.
 \]
Once $g_n$ is fixed, $K_{g_n}$ is bounded.  Choose all later masses so that
their total $K_{g_n}$ contribution is at most the same quantity.  A standard
diagonal choice, for example making each new mass smaller than one half of
every outstanding tail allowance, satisfies all these inequalities and
leaves a positive residual mass $a_0$.

By linearity of $\mathcal L_{g_n}$ before normalization, the $n$th cluster
contributes exactly
$a_nZ_{g_n}(\sigma_n)\mu_n$, while the combined past and future
contributions have at most $2\varepsilon_n$ times its mass.  Hence
\[
 \|\mathcal T_{g_n}\nu-\mu_n\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \leq4\varepsilon_n.
\]
Since $\mu_n\to m$ weakly, this proves
\eqref{eq:mobius-orbit-to-haar}.

For fixed $M$, the functional on the left of
\eqref{eq:no-uniform-mobius-history-contraction} is weakly continuous in
its measure argument: every finite product kernel is continuous, and it is
bounded by a constant depending only on $(M,\rho,\beta)$.  At Haar measure,
Poisson normalization gives
\[
 \int_\T|B_Mh+\overline{A_M}|^{-2}\dd m(h)=1
\]
for every phase history.  The functional therefore converges to one along
$\mathcal T_{g_n}\nu$.  The physical Hardy identity and concavity bound it
above by one, proving~\eqref{eq:no-uniform-mobius-history-contraction}.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Finite spectral model for a finite probe bundle]
\label{lem:finite-spectral-model}
Let $H$ be unitary on a separable Hilbert space and let
$x_1,\ldots,x_N$ be vectors.  Given an integer $T$ and $\varepsilon>0$, there
are a finite-dimensional subspace $\mathcal E$, a unitary
$\widehat H\in\U(\mathcal E)$, and vectors $\widehat x_p\in\mathcal E$ such
that every scalar reciprocal recursion of length at most $T$, for every phase
history in $\T^T$, has the same vector norms and $\beta$-fractional norms as
the recursion from $(H,x_p)$ up to error $\varepsilon$.  If $H(k)$ and
$x_p(k)$ are norm-continuous, the same model works on a sufficiently small
energy neighbourhood of any fixed $k_\star$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Partition $\T$ into finitely many arcs $\Gamma_s$ of chord diameter at most
$\delta$.  With $E_H$ the spectral resolution of $H$, put
\[
 \mathcal E_0=\operatorname{span}
 \{E_H(\Gamma_s)x_p:s,p\},
 \qquad
 \widehat H_0|_{E_H(\Gamma_s)\mathcal E_0}=\lambda_s\Id,
\]
where $\lambda_s\in\Gamma_s$.  The arc subspaces are orthogonal,
$\widehat H_0$ is unitary, and
\begin{equation}
 \|(H-\widehat H_0)y\|\leq\delta\|y\|,
 \qquad y\in\mathcal E_0.
 \label{eq:finite-spectral-residual}
\end{equation}
Every denominator $a\Id+zbH$ has inverse norm at most $e^\rho$.
Repeated use of the resolvent identity therefore bounds the difference of all
recursions of length at most $T$ by $C_{\rho,T}\delta$, uniformly in the phase
history.  Choose $\delta$ accordingly.  Approximate an orthonormal basis of
$\mathcal E_0$ and its conjugates by smooth angular vectors and polar-correct
the Gram matrix; one finite safety enlargement changes the preceding bound by
an arbitrarily small amount.  This gives $\mathcal E,\widehat H$ and
$\widehat x_p$.  Norm continuity of the data and the same resolvent estimate
give the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[One-window physical grouped contraction]
\label{prop:physical-grouped-one-window}
Let $W$ be a smooth real finite partial potential, let
$K\Subset(0,\infty)$, fix $k_\star\in K$, and fix finitely many compact probes.  Given
$\zeta>0$, finitely many protected fractional moments, finitely many
continuous spectral tests, and a prescribed inner radius, there are a number
$h>0$ and a finite group of smooth real scalar reciprocal cells outside that
radius such that, with $I=K\cap(k_\star-h,k_\star+h)$,
\begin{enumerate}
 \item the sum of the targeted $\beta$-fractional moments on $I$ is contracted
 by a factor at most $1/2$, up to error $\zeta$;
 \item every protected fractional moment is nonexpansive up to $\zeta$, and
 every protected spectral test changes by at most $\zeta$;
 \item the group has $X_3$ cost at most $\zeta$ and a pointwise
 amplitude bound depending only on the fixed hyperbolic height $\rho$.
\end{enumerate}
The contraction factor is independent of the resolved angular dimension, but
no uniform lower bound for $h$ is asserted.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Fix the hyperbolic height $\rho$ once and for all, and let $x_p(k)$ be the
finitely many current targeted radiation vectors.
Choose $\tau>0$ so small that the total initial fractional moment on the sets
where $\|x_p(k)\|<\tau$ is below the allocated error.  On the closed sets in
$K$ where $\|x_p(k)\|\geq\tau$, the normalized pairs
\[
 \left(\Theta_W(k),\frac{x_p(k)}{\|x_p(k)\|}\right)
\]
form a compact bundle.  Proposition~\ref{rem:phase-criterion} says that every
associated spectral measure is singular.  Theorem
~\ref{thm:singular-bundle-grouped-contraction}, with $q=1/2$, therefore gives
one finite group length $M$ valid uniformly on all of $K$, before any angular
cutoff is chosen.  The exact
phase-mean nonexpansion in Lemma~\ref{lem:physical-hardy} controls the omitted
small-norm part, so the ideal group contracts the full targeted moment on
any energy subinterval by at most $1/2$ plus its allocated error.

Now, and only now, apply Lemma~\ref{lem:finite-spectral-model} at the prescribed
$k_\star$ with the already fixed recursion length $T=M$.  Norm continuity
gives a neighbourhood $I=K\cap(k_\star-h,k_\star+h)$, with $h>0$, on which
that model and its residual estimates remain valid.  Proposition
~\ref{prop:full-block-talbot} makes the transmission and reflection
coefficients of the $M$ cells arbitrarily close to
$(a\Id,b\Id)$ on the resulting finite model.  Since the group and its phase
torus are finite, all ideal inverse outputs form a compact bundle.  Lemma
~\ref{lem:compact-output-stability}, the reflection gap in Lemma
~\ref{lem:reflection-gap}, and one finite safety enlargement transfer
\eqref{eq:singular-vector-bundle-contraction} to the exact physical outputs.
There is no dimension--length fixed point: $M$ was selected from the
infinite-dimensional singular spectral bundle before the finite model was
introduced.

Choose the free collar frequencies successively.  Lemma
~\ref{lem:one-step-placement} makes each targeted probe integral on $I$
agree, to its allocated error, with its $\T^M$ mean.  In the same
placement step include every protected fractional moment on $K$ and every
protected continuous test.  Lemma
~\ref{lem:physical-hardy} gives
nonexpansion or exact martingale preservation for their torus means.  Thus the
group contracts the current targeted moment on $I$ and protects every listed
quantity on $K$, up to its assigned error.

The group length $M$ is finite.  A depth-$m$ realization has total reflector
cost at most $MC_\rho e^{-cm}$, which tends to zero.  Choose $m$ large and
allocate the weak-slab, placement, and residual errors cell by cell.  Lemmas
~\ref{lem:scale-compatibility} and~\ref{lem:window-cost} meet these finite
requests.  Finally take the
active cutoff and then the outgoing Galerkin buffer as in
\eqref{eq:physical-buffer-target}.  Because all of these choices follow the
already fixed $M$, the outgoing buffer introduces no circular dependence.
This proves the three assertions.
\end{proof}

\begin{problem}[Global physical grouped Lemma B]
\label{prob:physical-grouped-global}
Under the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:physical-grouped-one-window},
prove that finitely many such groups can be assembled into one generation
which contracts every targeted fractional moment on all of $K$ by a factor at
most $3/4$, preserves the finite protected list up to $\zeta$, and has total
$X_3$ cost at most $\zeta$ with one pointwise amplitude bound.

Theorem~\ref{thm:singular-bundle-grouped-contraction} removes the angular
dimension and finite-model circularities from each individual window.  The
remaining issue is global chromatic coverage: groups aimed at earlier windows
change the reciprocal phase seen by later ones, and the resulting positive
window widths are not presently known to have a finite-cover lower bound.
Equivalently, it is enough to prove the hazard divergence condition
\eqref{eq:hazard-divergence} below, or a finite broadband scalar synthesis
which covers $K$ in one generation.
\end{problem}

\begin{proposition}[Adaptive centre averaging and the exact hazard]
\label{prop:adaptive-centre-averaging}
Let $K=[k_0,k_1]$, $|K|>0$, and let $f\in L^1(K)$ be nonnegative.  Fix
$0<h\leq |K|/2$ and $0<\gamma<1$.  Suppose that, for every centre
$c\in K$, a phase-placed physical cell tuned on
\[
 I(c)=K\cap(c-h,c+h)
\]
can be chosen so that its updated fractional density $f_c^+$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \int_K f_c^+\dd k
 \leq \int_K f\dd k
       -\gamma\int_{I(c)}f\dd k+\varepsilon.
 \label{eq:one-centre-gain}
\end{equation}
Then some centre $c\in K$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \int_K f_c^+\dd k
 \leq
 \left(1-\frac{\gamma h}{|K|}\right)
 \int_K f\dd k+\varepsilon.
 \label{eq:adaptive-hazard-step}
\end{equation}
For an adaptive sequence of such steps, with parameters
$(\gamma_j,h_j,\varepsilon_j)$, one has
\begin{equation}
 I_N\leq I_0
  \exp\!\left(-\frac1{|K|}\sum_{j=1}^N\gamma_jh_j\right)
 +\sum_{j=1}^N\varepsilon_j
   \exp\!\left(-\frac1{|K|}
     \sum_{s=j+1}^N\gamma_sh_s\right),
 \label{eq:hazard-product-bound}
\end{equation}
where $I_j=\int_K f_j\dd k$.  In particular, summable errors and
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=1}^\infty\gamma_jh_j=\infty
 \label{eq:hazard-divergence}
\end{equation}
force $I_j\to0$ after the error tail is chosen in the usual diagonal way.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
For every $k\in K$, the set of centres $c\in K$ for which $k\in I(c)$ has
length at least $h$.  Tonelli therefore gives
\[
 \frac1{|K|}\int_K\!\left(\int_{I(c)}f(k)\dd k\right)\dd c
 \geq\frac{h}{|K|}\int_Kf(k)\dd k.
\]
Average~\eqref{eq:one-centre-gain} in $c$ and select one centre no worse
than the average.  This proves~\eqref{eq:adaptive-hazard-step}.  Iteration and
$1-x\leq e^{-x}$ give~\eqref{eq:hazard-product-bound}; the last assertion is
immediate.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[What the physical coverage problem has become]
\label{cor:exact-hazard-bottleneck}
Proposition~\ref{prop:physical-grouped-one-window}, the physical Poisson
identity, deterministic phase placement, and the compact-output residual
estimate give~\eqref{eq:one-centre-gain} with $f=w^\beta$ and a fixed gap
\begin{equation}
 \gamma_0=\tfrac12-o(1)>0.
 \label{eq:fixed-grouped-window-gap}
\end{equation}
Thus Proposition~\ref{prop:adaptive-centre-averaging} reduces the global
Problem~\ref{prob:physical-grouped-global} to the accumulated hazard
$\sum_j\gamma_jh_j$.  Theorem
~\ref{thm:singular-bundle-grouped-contraction} removes the former angular
dimension and finite-model fixed point, but it does not bound the group length
uniformly over all singular spectral measures.  Consequently it supplies no
uniform lower bound on $h_j$.  Proposition~\ref{prop:grouped-root-zeno} proves
that the currently available black-box estimates can have a summable
certified hazard.  The unresolved statement is precisely
\eqref{eq:hazard-divergence}, or an equivalent finite broadband scalar
synthesis.  No counterexample follows from the one-window theorem alone.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proposition}[Positive-flight recurrence cannot supply a broadband inverse]
\label{prop:positive-flight-no-recurrence}
Let $D$ be self-adjoint and suppose that it has orthonormal eigenvectors
$e_0,e_1$ with eigenvalues $0$ and $\lambda>0$.  Fix a nondegenerate compact
interval $S=[s_0,s_1]\Subset(0,\infty)$ and $\tau>0$.  Then there is
$c=c(S,\lambda,\tau)>0$ such that, for every $T\geq0$ and every function
$\chi:S\to\T$,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{s\in S}
 \left\|
  e^{-isTD}-\chi(s)e^{is\tau D}
 \right\|_{\operatorname{span}\{e_0,e_1\}\to\mathcal H}
 \geq c.
 \label{eq:no-uniform-positive-recurrence}
\end{equation}
Thus the usual compact-group recurrence argument at one energy does not turn
the physical positive Talbot drift into a signed control uniformly on an
energy interval, even modulo an energy-dependent scalar phase.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Put $a=\lambda(T+\tau)$.  Testing the two eigenvectors and using the triangle
inequality gives
\begin{equation}
 2\sup_{s\in S}
 \left\|
  e^{-isTD}-\chi(s)e^{is\tau D}
 \right\|
 \geq
 \sup_{s\in S}|e^{-ias}-1|.
 \label{eq:two-level-relative-phase-bound}
\end{equation}
If $a(s_1-s_0)\geq2\pi$, the phase interval
$[as_0,as_1]$ contains an odd multiple of $\pi$, so the right side is $2$.
On the remaining compact range
\[
 \lambda\tau\leq a\leq\frac{2\pi}{s_1-s_0},
\]
the continuous function
$a\mapsto\sup_{s\in S}|e^{-ias}-1|$ has a strictly positive minimum: a zero
would force $e^{-ias}=1$ on a nondegenerate interval and hence $a=0$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:two-level-relative-phase-bound} proves the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Fej\'er conjugation repairs the ideal two-level drift]
\label{prop:fejer-su2-inverse}
Let $X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{su}(2)$ be normalized so that
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{Ad}_{e^{\theta X}}Z
 =\cos\theta\,Z+\sin\theta\,Y.
 \label{eq:su2-adjoint-rotation}
\end{equation}
Fix a compact interval $S=[s_0,s_1]\Subset(0,\infty)$.  Suppose that the
available ensemble words may use signed mixer pulses
\begin{equation}
 e^{usX},\qquad u\in\mathbb R,
 \label{eq:signed-su2-mixer}
\end{equation}
and only forward drift flights
\begin{equation}
 e^{tsZ},\qquad t\geq0.
 \label{eq:forward-su2-drift}
\end{equation}
Then, for every $\tau>0$ and $\varepsilon>0$, a finite word
$\mathcal V_s$ made only from~\eqref{eq:signed-su2-mixer} and
\eqref{eq:forward-su2-drift} satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{s\in S}\|\mathcal V_s-e^{-\tau sZ}\|<\varepsilon.
 \label{eq:fejer-uniform-inverse}
\end{equation}
Thus an exact noncommuting signed mixer removes the positive-flight
semigroup obstruction on an arbitrary compact detuning band.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Choose $\delta>0$ so that
\[
 0<\delta s_0\leq\delta s\leq\delta s_1<2\pi
 \qquad(s\in S).
\]
The Fej\'er kernel is
\begin{equation}
 F_N(x)=
 \frac1{N+1}
 \left(\frac{\sin((N+1)x/2)}{\sin(x/2)}\right)^2
 =
 1+2\sum_{n=1}^N
 \left(1-\frac n{N+1}\right)\cos(nx).
 \label{eq:fejer-positive-cosine}
\end{equation}
Because $\delta S$ stays a positive distance from $2\pi\mathbb Z$,
$F_N(\delta s)\to0$ uniformly for $s\in S$.

Put $\alpha_{n,N}=1-n/(N+1)>0$ and
\[
 V_{n,\pm}(s)
 =
 \operatorname{Ad}_{e^{\pm n\delta sX}}(sZ).
\]
Equation~\eqref{eq:su2-adjoint-rotation} gives the exact cancellation
\begin{equation}
 G_N(s):=
 \sum_{n=1}^N\alpha_{n,N}
 \bigl(V_{n,+}(s)+V_{n,-}(s)\bigr)
 =
 s\bigl(F_N(\delta s)-1\bigr)Z.
 \label{eq:fejer-negative-generator}
\end{equation}
Hence $G_N\to-sZ$ uniformly on $S$.

Every positive-time flow of a summand is an admissible word:
\begin{equation}
 e^{t\alpha_{n,N}V_{n,\pm}(s)}
 =
 e^{\pm n\delta sX}
 e^{t\alpha_{n,N}sZ}
 e^{\mp n\delta sX},
 \qquad t\geq0.
 \label{eq:conjugated-forward-flight}
\end{equation}
For fixed $N$, the Lie product formula applied to the finite sum in
\eqref{eq:fejer-negative-generator} approximates $e^{\tau G_N(s)}$
uniformly in $s\in S$ by products of the admissible factors
\eqref{eq:conjugated-forward-flight}.  First choose $N$ so that
$e^{\tau G_N(s)}$ is uniformly close to $e^{-\tau sZ}$ and then choose the
Lie-product subdivision sufficiently fine.  This proves
\eqref{eq:fejer-uniform-inverse}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[No exact scalar Galerkin mixer]
\label{prop:no-exact-scalar-galerkin-mixer}
Let $\mathcal E\subset C^\infty(\mathbb S^2)$ be a nonzero finite-dimensional
subspace invariant under $A_3=-\Delta_{\mathbb S^2}$.  If a smooth real
function $q$ also satisfies
\begin{equation}
 M_q\mathcal E\subset\mathcal E,
 \label{eq:exact-multiplier-invariance}
\end{equation}
then $q$ is constant.  In particular, a nontrivial signed mixer for
